{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929","id":"msg_vrtx_01WV5a6yE92VAxmhdXEsbdim","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_vrtx_01H88nVeFhttfo1fZhXhdgnf","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/root/environment/skills/excel-lookup-functions.md","content":"# Excel Lookup Functions\n\n## Overview\nExcel lookup functions are used to search for values in tables and retrieve corresponding data. They are essential for cross-referencing data and performing two-way lookups.\n\n## Common Lookup Functions\n\n### 1. VLOOKUP (Vertical Lookup)\nSearches for a value in the first column of a range and returns a value in the same row from another column.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=VLOOKUP(lookup_value, table_array, col_index_num, [range_lookup])\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```\n=VLOOKUP(A2, DataSheet!A:D, 3, FALSE)\n```\n\n### 2. HLOOKUP (Horizontal Lookup)\nSearches for a value in the first row of a range and returns a value in the same column from another row.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=HLOOKUP(lookup_value, table_array, row_index_num, [range_lookup])\n```\n\n### 3. INDEX & MATCH (Two-Way Lookup)\nThe most flexible lookup combination that can search both vertically and horizontally.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=INDEX(return_range, MATCH(lookup_value1, lookup_range1, 0), MATCH(lookup_value2, lookup_range2, 0))\n```\n\n**Example - Two Criteria:**\n```\n=INDEX(Data!$A$1:$Z$100, MATCH(lookup_row, Data!$A$1:$A$100, 0), MATCH(lookup_col, Data!$A$1:$Z$1, 0))\n```\n\n### 4. XLOOKUP (Modern Alternative - Excel 365+)\nA newer function that combines features of VLOOKUP, HLOOKUP, and INDEX/MATCH.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=XLOOKUP(lookup_value, lookup_array, return_array, [if_not_found], [match_mode], [search_mode])\n```\n\n**With Two Criteria:**\n```\n=XLOOKUP(1, (criteria1=range1)*(criteria2=range2), return_range)\n```\n\n## Two-Criteria Lookup Best Practices\n\n### Using INDEX/MATCH for Two Conditions\nWhen you need to match both a row identifier and a column identifier:\n\n1. Use MATCH to find the row based on first criterion\n2. Use MATCH to find the column based on second criterion\n3. Use INDEX to return the intersection\n\n**Formula Pattern:**\n```\n=INDEX(data_range, MATCH(row_criterion, row_headers, 0), MATCH(col_criterion, col_headers, 0))\n```\n\n### Key Parameters\n- `0` or `FALSE`: Exact match (recommended for most cases)\n- `1` or `TRUE`: Approximate match (requires sorted data)\n- `-1`: Find smallest value greater than or equal to lookup value\n\n## Common Use Cases\n\n### Cross-referencing with Series Code and Year\n```\n=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))\n```\n\nThis formula:\n- Searches in Data sheet, range A21:F40\n- Matches row based on series code in D12\n- Matches column based on year in H10\n- Returns the value at the intersection\n\n## Error Handling\n```\n=IFERROR(INDEX(MATCH(...)), \"Not Found\")\n```\n\n## Performance Tips\n- Use absolute references ($) for ranges that don't change\n- Use relative references for criteria that vary\n- Avoid volatile functions when possible\n- INDEX/MATCH is generally faster than VLOOKUP for large datasets\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":5,"cache_creation_input_tokens":337,"cache_read_input_tokens":18920,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":337,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"7e740bfe-08df-480e-b76c-6010c6a8e1ca","uuid":"8e00fb26-aa2b-4f6a-9635-d6ae7429cc88"}

MIN - Minimum Value\nReturns the smallest value in a range.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=MIN(number1, [number2], ...)\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```\n=MIN(A1:A10)\n=MIN(A1:E1)\n```\n\n### 2. MAX - Maximum Value\nReturns the largest value in a range.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=MAX(number1, [number2], ...)\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```\n=MAX(A1:A10)\n```\n\n### 3. MEDIAN - Middle Value\nReturns the median (middle value) of a set of numbers.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=MEDIAN(number1, [number2], ...)\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```\n=MEDIAN(A1:A10)\n```\n\n**Notes:**\n- If even count: average of two middle values\n- If odd count: the middle value\n\n### 4. AVERAGE (Mean) - Arithmetic Mean\nReturns the average of a range of numbers.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=AVERAGE(number1, [number2], ...)\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```\n=AVERAGE(A1:A10)\n```\n\n### 5. PERCENTILE Functions\n\n#### PERCENTILE.INC (Inclusive)\nReturns the k-th percentile of values (includes 0 and 1).\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=PERCENTILE.INC(array, k)\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```\n=PERCENTILE.INC(A1:A10, 0.25)  ' 25th percentile\n=PERCENTILE.INC(A1:A10, 0.75)  ' 75th percentile\n```\n\n#### PERCENTILE.EXC (Exclusive)\nReturns the k-th percentile (excludes 0 and 1).\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=PERCENTILE.EXC(array, k)\n```\n\n**Notes:**\n- k must be between 0 and 1\n- 0.25 = 25th percentile (Q1)\n- 0.50 = 50th percentile (median)\n- 0.75 = 75th percentile (Q3)\n\n#### QUARTILE Function\n```\n=QUARTILE.INC(array, quart)\n```\n- quart = 1: 25th percentile\n- quart = 2: 50th percentile (median)\n- quart = 3: 75th percentile\n\n## Advanced Functions\n\n### SUMPRODUCT - Weighted Calculations\nMultiplies corresponding components in arrays and returns the sum of those products.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=SUMPRODUCT(array1, [array2], ...)\n```\n\n**Example - Simple Product Sum:**\n```\n=SUMPRODUCT(A1:A5, B1:B5)\n' Equivalent to: A1*B1 + A2*B2 + A3*B3 + A4*B4 + A5*B5\n```\n\n**Example - Weighted Average:**\n```\n=SUMPRODUCT(values, weights) / SUM(weights)\n```\n\n**Example - Weighted Mean:**\n```\n=SUMPRODUCT(H35:L35, H7:L7) / SUM(H7:L7)\n```\n\nThis calculates:\n- Multiply each value by its weight\n- Sum all products\n- Divide by sum of weights\n\n### COUNT and COUNTA\n```\n=COUNT(range)      ' Counts numbers only\n=COUNTA(range)     ' Counts non-empty cells\n```\n\n## Working with Multiple Ranges\n\n### Horizontal Ranges\n```\n=AVERAGE(A1:E1)    ' Calculate across columns\n=MIN(A1:E1)\n=MAX(A1:E1)\n```\n\n### Vertical Ranges\n```\n=AVERAGE(A1:A10)   ' Calculate down rows\n=MEDIAN(A1:A10)\n```\n\n### 2D Ranges\n```\n=AVERAGE(A1:E10)   ' Calculate across entire block\n```\n\n## Practical Examples\n\n### Calculate Statistics for a Row of Data\n```\n' Given data in H35:L35\n=MIN(H35:L35)               ' Minimum\n=MAX(H35:L35)               ' Maximum\n=MEDIAN(H35:L35)            ' Median\n=AVERAGE(H35:L35)           ' Mean\n=PERCENTILE.INC(H35:L35, 0.25)  ' 25th percentile\n=PERCENTILE.INC(H35:L35, 0.75)  ' 75th percentile\n```\n\n### Weighted Mean Calculation\n```\n' Values in H35:L40, Weights in H7:L7\n=SUMPRODUCT(H35:L40, H7:L7) / SUM(H7:L7)\n```\n\n## Common Errors\n- `#DIV/0!`: Division by zero (check SUM of weights)\n- `#VALUE!`: Non-numeric data in range\n- `#NUM!`: Invalid percentile value (must be 0-1)\n- `#N/A`: Missing or invalid data\n\n## Best Practices\n1. Use absolute references ($) for fixed ranges\n2. Use relative references for formulas that will be copied\n3. Verify data types (numbers vs text)\n4. Check for blank cells or errors in ranges\n5. Use IFERROR() for error handling when needed\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":5,"cache_creation_input_tokens":337,"cache_read_input_tokens":18920,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":337,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"7e740bfe-08df-480e-b76c-6010c6a8e1ca","uuid":"2cfa3a0b-27d4-4faf-9ff9-ffea4d1dd80c"}

It allows programmatic manipulation of Excel workbooks while preserving formatting, formulas, and styles.\n\n## Installation\n```bash\npip install openpyxl\n```\n\n## Basic Usage\n\n### Loading an Existing Workbook\n```python\nfrom openpyxl import load_workbook\n\n# Load workbook\nwb = load_workbook('filename.xlsx')\n\n# Access sheets\nsheet = wb['SheetName']  # By name\nsheet = wb.active        # Active sheet\n```\n\n### Reading Cell Values\n```python\n# Access by cell reference\nvalue = sheet['A1'].value\n\n# Access by row and column (1-indexed)\nvalue = sheet.cell(row=1, column=1).value\n```\n\n### Writing Cell Values\n```python\n# Write a value\nsheet['A1'] = 'Hello'\nsheet['B2'] = 123\n\n# Write using row/column\nsheet.cell(row=1, column=1, value='Hello')\n```\n\n### Writing Formulas\n```python\n# Write a formula (starts with =)\nsheet['C1'] = '=A1+B1'\nsheet['D5'] = '=SUM(A1:A10)'\nsheet['E10'] = '=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))'\n\n# Excel formulas are stored as strings\n# They are evaluated when the file is opened in Excel\n```\n\n## Working with Multiple Sheets\n```python\n# List all sheet names\nprint(wb.sheetnames)\n\n# Access specific sheet\ndata_sheet = wb['Data']\ntask_sheet = wb['Task']\n\n# Switch between sheets\nfor sheet_name in wb.sheetnames:\n    sheet = wb[sheet_name]\n    print(f\"Working on {sheet_name}\")\n```\n\n## Cell Ranges\n```python\n# Read a range\nfor row in sheet['A1:C3']:\n    for cell in row:\n        print(cell.value)\n\n# Iterate rows\nfor row in sheet.iter_rows(min_row=1, max_row=10, min_col=1, max_col=5):\n    for cell in row:\n        print(cell.value)\n\n# Get values only (faster)\nfor row in sheet.iter_rows(min_row=1, max_row=10, values_only=True):\n    print(row)\n```\n\n## Preserving Formatting\n```python\n# openpyxl preserves formatting by default\n# Just load, modify, and save\n\nwb = load_workbook('filename.xlsx')\nsheet = wb['Task']\n\n# Make changes (formatting is preserved)\nsheet['A1'] = '=SUM(B1:B10)'\n\n# Save (all original formatting retained)\nwb.save('filename.xlsx')\n```\n\n## Cell References in Formulas\n```python\n# Absolute reference ($A$1)\nsheet['B2'] = '=INDEX($A$1:$Z$100, MATCH($D12, $A$1:$A$100, 0), 2)'\n\n# Relative =INDEX(data_range, MATCH(row_criteria, row_range, 0), MATCH(col_criteria, col_range, 0))\nformula = '=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))'\nsheet['H12'] = formula\n```\n\n### Statistical Functions\n```python\n# MIN, MAX, MEDIAN, AVERAGE\nsheet['M35'] = '=MIN(H35:L35)'\nsheet['M36'] = '=MAX(H35:L35)'\nsheet['M37'] = file\nwb.save('filename.xlsx')\n\n# Save to different file\nwb.save('output.xlsx')\n\n# Close workbook (optional, good practice)\nwb.close()\n```\n\n## Best Practices\n\n### 1. Preserve Original Formatting\n```python\n# Don't modify styles unless necessary\n# openpyxl preserves formatting automatically\nwb = load_workbook('file.xlsx')\nsheet['A1'] = 'New Value'  # Keeps original formatting\nwb.save('file.xlsx')\n```\n\n### 2. Work with Existing Structure\n```python\n# Don't add/remove sheets unless necessary\n# Don't modify cell styles/colors\n# Only change values and formulas\n```\n\n### 4. Formula Copying Pattern\n```python\n# When copying formulas to multiple cells\nbase_formula = '=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D{}, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH({}$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))'\n\nfor row in range(12, 18):  # rows 12-17\n    for col in ['H', 'I', 'J', 'K', 'L']:\n        formula = base_formula.format(row, col)\n        sheet[f'{col}{row}'] = formula\n```\n\n## Error Handling\n```python\nfrom openpyxl import load_workbook\nfrom openpyxl.utils.exceptions import InvalidFileException\n\ntry:\n    wb = load_workbook('file.xlsx')\nexcept FileNotFoundError:\n    print(\"File not found\")\nexcept InvalidFileException:\n    print(\"Invalid Excel file\")\n```\n\n## Example: Complete Workflow\n```python\nfrom openpyxl import load_workbook\n\n# Load workbook\nwb = load_workbook('gdp.xlsx')\ntask_sheet = wb['Task']\ndata_sheet = wb['Data']\n\n# Write formulas\ntask_sheet['H12'] = '=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))'\n\n# Write statistical formulas\ntask_sheet['M35'] = '=MIN(H35:L35)'\ntask_sheet['M36'] = '=AVERAGE(H35:L35)'\n\n# Write weighted mean\ntask_sheet['H42'] = '=SUMPRODUCT(H35:L40, H7:L7) / SUM(H7:L7)'\n\n# Save and close\nwb.save('gdp.xlsx')\nwb.close()\n```\n\n## Common Issues\n- Formulas are stored as text, calculated when opened in Excel\n- Always use `=` prefix for formulas\n- Use proper Excel cell reference syntax\n- openpyxl doesn't calculate formulas (Excel does)\n- To see results, open file in Excel after saving\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":5,"cache_creation_input_tokens":337,"cache_read_input_tokens":18920,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":337,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"7e740bfe-08df-480e-b76c-6010c6a8e1ca","uuid":"a5c1a81e-8109-4ff0-8328-7379e1d1c908"}

Understanding GDP components and related metrics is essential for economic analysis.\n\n## GDP Components\n\n### Basic GDP Equation\n```\nGDP = C + I + G + (X - M)\n```\n\nWhere:\n- **C** = Consumption (household spending)\n- **I** = Investment (business investment)\n- **G** = Government spending\n- **X** = Exports\n- **M** = Imports\n- **(X - M)** = Net Exports (trade balance)\n\n## Net Exports\n\n### Definition\nNet exports represent the difference between a country's exports and imports.\n\n```\nNet Exports = Exports - Imports\n```\n\n### Net Exports as % of GDP\n```\nNet Exports (% of GDP) = [(Exports - Imports) / GDP] × 100\n```\n\n### Interpretation\n- **Positive Net Exports**: Trade surplus (exports > imports)\n- **Negative Net Exports**: Trade deficit (imports > exports)\n- **Zero Net Exports**: Trade balance\n\n## GCC Countries (Gulf Cooperation Council)\n\nThe GCC consists of six countries:\n1. **Bahrain** (BHR)\n2. **Kuwait** (KWT)\n3. **Oman** (OMN)\n4. **Qatar** (QAT)\n5. **Saudi Arabia** (SAU)\n6. **United Arab Emirates** (ARE)\n\nThese are oil-rich nations with historically strong export sectors.\n\n## Weighted Mean Calculation\n\n### Simple Mean vs Weighted Mean\n\n**Simple Mean:**\n```\nMean = (x₁ + x₂ + ... + xₙ) / n\n```\n\n**Weighted Mean:**\n```\nWeighted Mean = (x₁×w₁ + x₂×w₂ + ... + xₙ×wₙ) / (w₁ + w₂ + ... + wₙ)\n```\n\n### GDP-Weighted Mean\n\nWhen calculating regional averages, GDP is commonly used as the weight:\n\n```\nWeighted Mean = Σ(Country_Value × Country_GDP) / Σ(Country_GDP)\n```\n\n**Example:**\nIf calculating GCC's average net exports as % of GDP:\n```\nGCC_Weighted_Mean =\n  (Bahrain_NetExp% × Bahrain_GDP +\n   Kuwait_NetExp% × Kuwait_GDP +\n   ... +\n   UAE_NetExp% × UAE_GDP) /\n  (Sum of all GCC countries' GDP)\n```\n\n### Using SUMPRODUCT in Excel\n```excel\n=SUMPRODUCT(net_exports_range, gdp_range) / SUM(gdp_range)\n```\n\nWhere:\n- `net_exports_range`: Net exports as % of GDP for all countries\n- `gdp_range`: GDP values for all countries (weights)\n\n## Statistical Measures for Economic Analysis\n\n### Central Tendency\n- **Mean**: Average value (simple or weighted)\n- **Median**: Middle value (less affected by outliers)\n\n### Dispersion\n- **Min**: Lowest value\n- **Max**: Highest value\n- **Range**: Max - Min\n\n### Distribution\n- **25th Percentile (Q1)**: 25% of values are below this\n- **75th Percentile (Q3)**: 75% of values are below this\n- **Interquartile Range (IQR)**: Q3 - Q1\n\n## Series Codes (Common Economic Indicators)\n\nTypical series codes in economic data:\n- **NY.GDP.MKTP.CD**: GDP (current US$)\n- **NE.EXP.GNFS.ZS**: Exports of goods and services (% of GDP)\n- **NE.IMP.GNFS.ZS**: Imports of goods and services (% of GDP)\n- **NE.RSB.GNFS.ZS**: External balance on goods and services (% of GDP)\n- **GDP**: Gross Domestic Product\n- **EXP**: Exports\n- **IMP**: Imports\n\n## Practical Application\n\n### Step 1: Data Lookup\nRetrieve economic indicators using series codes and years:\n```\nINDEX(data_range, MATCH(series_code, codes, 0), MATCH(year, years, 0))\n```\n\n### Step 2: Calculate Net Exports\n```\nNet Exports % = Exports % - Imports %\n```\n\nOr use the external balance series if available.\n\n### Step 3: Calculate Statistics\nFor each year across all countries:\n- Min, Max, Median, Mean\n- 25th and 75th percentiles\n\n### Step 4: Weighted Mean\nUse GDP as weights:\n```\nSUMPRODUCT(net_exports_values, gdp_values) / SUM(gdp_values)\n```\n\n## Why Weighted Mean Matters\n\n**Example Scenario:**\n- Country A: Net Exports = 10%, GDP = $10 billion\n- Country B: Net Exports = 30%, GDP = $100 billion\n\n**Simple Mean:** (10% + 30%) / 2 = 20%\n\n**Weighted Mean:**\n(10% × 10 + 30% × 100) / (10 + 100) = 3100 / 110 = 28.2%\n\nThe weighted mean gives more importance to Country B because its economy is 10× larger, providing a more accurate regional picture.\n\n## Key Considerations\n\n1. **Currency**: Ensure GDP is in same currency across countries\n2. **Time Period**: Use same years for comparison\n3. **Data Quality**: Verify source and completeness\n4. **Weights**: GDP weights reflect economic size importance\n5. **Interpretation**: Weighted mean represents economic reality better than simple mean for regional aggregates\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":5,"cache_creation_input_tokens":337,"cache_read_input_tokens":18920,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":337,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"7e740bfe-08df-480e-b76c-6010c6a8e1ca","uuid":"1dc97944-0537-4da6-94fc-55ff03d67374"}

Understanding absolute, relative, and mixed references is crucial for building formulas that work correctly when copied to other cells.\n\n## Types of Cell References\n\n### 1. Relative Reference (A1)\nChanges when formula is copied to another cell.\n\n**Example:**\n```\nA1\n```\n\n**Behavior:**\n- Copied right: becomes B1, C1, D1, etc.\n- Copied down: becomes A2, A3, A4, etc.\n- Copied diagonally: both row and column adjust\n\n**Use Case:** When you want the reference to adjust relative to the new position.\n\n### 2. Absolute Reference ($A$1)\nStays fixed when formula is copied anywhere.\n\n**Example:**\n```\n$A$1\n```\n\n**Behavior:**\n- Always refers to cell A1, no matter where copied\n- Both row (1) and column (A) are locked\n\n**Use Case:** Referring to a constant value, fixed range, or lookup table.\n\n### 3. Mixed References ($A1 or A$1)\n\n**Column Absolute, Row Relative ($A1):**\n```\n$A1\n```\n- Column A is locked\n- Row changes when copied up/down\n- Useful for formulas copied down but referencing fixed column\n\n**Row Absolute, Column Relative (A$1):**\n```\nA$1\n```\n- Row 1 is locked\n- Column changes when copied left/right\n- Useful for formulas copied across but referencing fixed row\n\n## Formula Copying Patterns\n\n### Pattern 1: Two-Way Lookup Table\nCommon in data retrieval where rows and columns represent different criteria.\n\n**Structure:**\n```\n        H       I       J       K       L\n10            2019    2020    2021    2022    (Column headers)\n12   CODE1    ???     ???     ???     ???\n13   CODE2    ???     ???     ???     ???\n```\n\n**Formula for H12:**\n```\n=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))\n```\n\n**Breaking it down:**\n- `Data!$A$21:$F$40`: Absolute data range (never changes)\n- `$D12`: Column locked, row relative (D12, D13, D14 when copied down)\n- `Data!$A$21:$A$40`: Absolute lookup range for rows\n- `H$10`: Row locked, column relative (H10, I10, J10 when copied right)\n- `Data!$A$21:$F$21`: Absolute lookup range for columns\n\n**When copied:**\n- To I12: becomes `MATCH(I$10, ...)` - different year\n- To H13: becomes `MATCH($D13, ...)` - different code\n- To I13: both adjust - different year AND code\n\n### Pattern 2: Statistical Calculations Across Rows\nCalculating statistics for each row separately.\n\n**Formula for M35 (MIN):**\n```\n=MIN(H35:L35)\n```\n\n**When copied to M36:**\n```\n=MIN(H36:L36)\n```\n\n**Use:** All references are relative, so the formula adjusts to each row.\n\n### Pattern 3: SUMPRODUCT with Fixed Weights\nWeighted calculation where weights don't change.\n\n**Formula:**\n```\n=SUMPRODUCT(H35:L35, H$7:L$7) / SUM(H$7:L$7)\n```\n\n**Breaking it down:**\n- `H35:L35`: Fully relative (values row changes when copied)\n- `H$7:L$7`: Row locked (weights always from row 7)\n- `SUM(H$7:L$7)`: Same - row locked to 7\n\n**When copied to row 36:**\n```\n=SUMPRODUCT(H36:L36, H$7:L$7) / SUM(H$7:L$7)\n```\nValues row changes, weights stay fixed.\n\n## Cross-Sheet References\n\n### Syntax\n```\nSheetName!CellReference\n```\n\n**Examples:**\n```\nData!A1              ' Cell A1 in Data sheet\nData!$A$1            ' Absolute reference to A1 in Data sheet\nData!$A$21:$F$40     ' Absolute range in Data sheet\n'Sheet Name'!A1      ' Use quotes if sheet name has spaces\n```\n\n### Best Practice for Cross-Sheet Formulas\n```\n=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))\n```\n\n- Always use absolute references for ranges in other sheets\n- Use appropriate relative/absolute for criteria based on copy direction\n\n## Common Formula Patterns\n\n### 1. Lookup with Two Criteria (Row and Column)\n```\n=INDEX(absolute_data_range,\n       MATCH(row_criterion_mixed, absolute_row_range, 0),\n       MATCH(col_criterion_mixed, absolute_col_range, 0))\n```\n\n### 2. Statistical Functions for Horizontal Range\n```\n=MIN(relative_range)      ' Each row gets its own calculation\n=MAX(relative_range)\n=MEDIAN(relative_range)\n=AVERAGE(relative_range)\n```\n\n### 3. Percentile Calculations\n```\n=PERCENTILE.INC(relative_range, 0.25)   ' 25th percentile\n=PERCENTILE.INC(relative_range, 0.75)   ' 75th percentile\n```\n\n### 4. Weighted Mean with Fixed Weights\n```\n=SUMPRODUCT(relative_values, absolute_weights) / SUM(absolute_weights)\n```\n\n## Quick Reference Guide\n\n| Pattern | Example | Behavior When Copied |\n|---------|---------|----------------------|\n| Relative | A1 | Both row and column adjust |\n| Absolute | $A$1 | Nothing changes |\n| Column Locked | $A1 | Column fixed, row adjusts |\n| Row Locked | A$1 | Row fixed, column adjusts |\n| Range Absolute | $A$1:$B$10 | Range stays fixed |\n| Cross-sheet | Data!$A$1 | Sheet reference maintained |\n\n## Practical Example: Building a Formula Grid\n\n**Scenario:** Fill H12:L17 with lookups based on column D (codes) and row 10 (years).\n\n**Step 1 - Write formula for H12:**\n```\n=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))\n```\n\n**Step 2 - Copy across to I12:L12:**\n- H$10 becomes I$10, J$10, K$10, L$10 (different years)\n- $D12 stays as $D12 (same row code)\n\n**Step 3 - Copy entire row down to rows 13-17:**\n- $D12 becomes $D13, $D14, $D15, etc. (different codes)\n- Column references maintain their pattern\n\n**Result:** Full grid automatically populated with correct lookups.\n\n## Debugging Tips\n\n1. **Formula Auditing:**\n   - Click on cell to see formula in formula bar\n   - Check if $ symbols are in right places\n   - Verify sheet references\n\n2. **Trace Precedents:**\n   - Use Excel's \"Trace Precedents\" feature\n   - Verify cells being referenced\n\n3. **Test Pattern:**\n   - Write formula for one cell\n   - Copy to adjacent cell\n   - Check if references adjusted correctly\n   - If correct, copy to entire range\n\n4. **Common Errors:**\n   - `#REF!`: Invalid cell reference (often from incorrect absolute/relative)\n   - `#N/A`: MATCH couldn't find value (check criteria)\n   - `#VALUE!`: Wrong data type in formula\n"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":5,"cache_creation_input_tokens":337,"cache_read_input_tokens":18920,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":337,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"7e740bfe-08df-480e-b76c-6010c6a8e1ca","uuid":"46c33971-c9fb-4de2-9653-e2d2fe733c90"}

They are essential for cross-referencing data and performing two-way lookups.\n\n## Common Lookup Functions\n\n### 1. VLOOKUP (Vertical Lookup)\nSearches for a value in the first column of a range and returns a value in the same row from another column.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=VLOOKUP(lookup_value, table_array, col_index_num, [range_lookup])\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```\n=VLOOKUP(A2, DataSheet!A:D, 3, FALSE)\n```\n\n### 2. HLOOKUP (Horizontal Lookup)\nSearches for a value in the first row of a range and returns a value in the same column from another row.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=HLOOKUP(lookup_value, table_array, row_index_num, [range_lookup])\n```\n\n### 3. INDEX & MATCH (Two-Way Lookup)\nThe most flexible lookup combination that can search both vertically and horizontally.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=INDEX(return_range, MATCH(lookup_value1, lookup_range1, 0), MATCH(lookup_value2, lookup_range2, 0))\n```\n\n**Example - Two Criteria:**\n```\n=INDEX(Data!$A$1:$Z$100, MATCH(lookup_row, Data!$A$1:$A$100, 0), MATCH(lookup_col, Data!$A$1:$Z$1, 0))\n```\n\n### 4. XLOOKUP (Modern Alternative - Excel 365+)\nA newer function that combines features of VLOOKUP, HLOOKUP, and INDEX/MATCH.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=XLOOKUP(lookup_value, lookup_array, return_array, [if_not_found], [match_mode], [search_mode])\n```\n\n**With Two Criteria:**\n```\n=XLOOKUP(1, (criteria1=range1)*(criteria2=range2), return_range)\n```\n\n## Two-Criteria Lookup Best Practices\n\n### Using INDEX/MATCH for Two Conditions\nWhen you need to match both a row identifier and a column identifier:\n\n1. Use MATCH to find the row based on first criterion\n2. Use MATCH to find the column based on second criterion\n3. Use INDEX to return the intersection\n\n**Formula Pattern:**\n```\n=INDEX(data_range, MATCH(row_criterion, row_headers, 0), MATCH(col_criterion, col_headers, 0))\n```\n\n### Key Parameters\n- `0` or `FALSE`: Exact match (recommended for most cases)\n- `1` or `TRUE`: Approximate match (requires sorted data)\n- `-1`: Find smallest value greater than or equal to lookup value\n\n## Common Use Cases\n\n### Cross-referencing with Series Code and Year\n```\n=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))\n```\n\nThis formula:\n- Searches in Data sheet, range A21:F40\n- Matches row based on series code in D12\n- Matches column based on year in H10\n- Returns the value at the intersection\n\n## Error Handling\n```\n=IFERROR(INDEX(MATCH(...)), \"Not Found\")\n```\n\n## Performance Tips\n- Use absolute references ($) for ranges that don't change\n- Use relative references for criteria that vary\n- Avoid volatile functions when possible\n- INDEX/MATCH is generally faster than VLOOKUP for large datasets\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}

MIN - Minimum Value\nReturns the smallest value in a range.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=MIN(number1, [number2], ...)\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```\n=MIN(A1:A10)\n=MIN(A1:E1)\n```\n\n### 2. MAX - Maximum Value\nReturns the largest value in a range.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=MAX(number1, [number2], ...)\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```\n=MAX(A1:A10)\n```\n\n### 3. MEDIAN - Middle Value\nReturns the median (middle value) of a set of numbers.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=MEDIAN(number1, [number2], ...)\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```\n=MEDIAN(A1:A10)\n```\n\n**Notes:**\n- If even count: average of two middle values\n- If odd count: the middle value\n\n### 4. AVERAGE (Mean) - Arithmetic Mean\nReturns the average of a range of numbers.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=AVERAGE(number1, [number2], ...)\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```\n=AVERAGE(A1:A10)\n```\n\n### 5. PERCENTILE Functions\n\n#### PERCENTILE.INC (Inclusive)\nReturns the k-th percentile of values (includes 0 and 1).\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=PERCENTILE.INC(array, k)\n```\n\n**Example:**\n```\n=PERCENTILE.INC(A1:A10, 0.25)  ' 25th percentile\n=PERCENTILE.INC(A1:A10, 0.75)  ' 75th percentile\n```\n\n#### PERCENTILE.EXC (Exclusive)\nReturns the k-th percentile (excludes 0 and 1).\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=PERCENTILE.EXC(array, k)\n```\n\n**Notes:**\n- k must be between 0 and 1\n- 0.25 = 25th percentile (Q1)\n- 0.50 = 50th percentile (median)\n- 0.75 = 75th percentile (Q3)\n\n#### QUARTILE Function\n```\n=QUARTILE.INC(array, quart)\n```\n- quart = 1: 25th percentile\n- quart = 2: 50th percentile (median)\n- quart = 3: 75th percentile\n\n## Advanced Functions\n\n### SUMPRODUCT - Weighted Calculations\nMultiplies corresponding components in arrays and returns the sum of those products.\n\n**Syntax:**\n```\n=SUMPRODUCT(array1, [array2], ...)\n```\n\n**Example - Simple Product Sum:**\n```\n=SUMPRODUCT(A1:A5, B1:B5)\n' Equivalent to: A1*B1 + A2*B2 + A3*B3 + A4*B4 + A5*B5\n```\n\n**Example - It allows programmatic manipulation of Excel workbooks while preserving formatting, formulas, and styles.\n\n## Installation\n```bash\npip install openpyxl\n```\n\n## Basic Usage\n\n### Loading an Existing Workbook\n```python\nfrom openpyxl import load_workbook\n\n# Load workbook\nwb = load_workbook('filename.xlsx')\n\n# Access sheets\nsheet = wb['SheetName']  # By name\nsheet = wb.active        # Active sheet\n```\n\n### Reading Cell Values\n```python\n# Access by cell reference\nvalue = sheet['A1'].value\n\n# Access by row and column (1-indexed)\nvalue = sheet.cell(row=1, value='Hello')\n```\n\n### Writing Formulas\n```python\n# Write a formula (starts with =)\nsheet['C1'] = '=A1+B1'\nsheet['D5'] = '=SUM(A1:A10)'\nsheet['E10'] = '=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))'\n\n# Excel formulas are stored as strings\n# They are evaluated when the file is opened in Excel\n```\n\n## Working with Multiple print(cell.value)\n\n# Iterate rows\nfor row in sheet.iter_rows(min_row=1, max_row=10, min_col=1, max_col=5):\n    for cell in row:\n        print(cell.value)\n\n# Get values only (faster)\nfor row in sheet.iter_rows(min_row=1, max_row=10, values_only=True):\n    print(row)\n```\n\n## Preserving Formatting\n```python\n# openpyxl preserves formatting by default\n# Just load, modify, and save\n\nwb = retained)\nwb.save('filename.xlsx')\n```\n\n## Cell References in Formulas\n```python\n# Absolute reference ($A$1)\nsheet['B2'] = '=INDEX($A$1:$Z$100, MATCH($D12, $A$1:$A$100, 0), 2)'\n\n# Relative =INDEX(data_range, MATCH(row_criteria, row_range, 0), MATCH(col_criteria, col_range, 0))\nformula = '=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))'\nsheet['H12'] = formula\n```\n\n### Statistical Functions\n```python\n# MIN, MAX, MEDIAN, AVERAGE\nsheet['M35'] = '=MIN(H35:L35)'\nsheet['M36'] = '=MAX(H35:L35)'\nsheet['M37'] = '=MEDIAN(H35:L35)'\nsheet['M38'] = '=AVERAGE(H35:L35)'\n\n# PERCENTILE\nsheet['M39'] = '=PERCENTILE.INC(H35:L35, 0.25)'\nsheet['M40'] = '=PERCENTILE.INC(H35:L35, 0.75)'\n```\n\n### SUMPRODUCT for Weighted Mean\n```python\n# =SUMPRODUCT(values, weights) / SUM(weights)\nsheet['N42'] = '=SUMPRODUCT(H35:L40, H7:L7) / SUM(H7:L7)'\n```\n\n## Saving Files\n```python\n# Save to same file\nwb.save('filename.xlsx')\n\n# Save to different file\nwb.save('output.xlsx')\n\n# Close workbook (optional, good practice)\nwb.close()\n```\n\n## Best Practices\n\n### 1. Preserve Original Formatting\n```python\n# Don't modify styles unless necessary\n# openpyxl preserves formatting automatically\nwb = load_workbook('file.xlsx')\nsheet['A1'] = 'New Value'  # Keeps original formatting\nwb.save('file.xlsx')\n```\n\n### 2. Use Proper Cell References\n```python\n# Use Excel-style references in formulas\nsheet['A1'] = '=B1+C1'  # Good\n# Don't try to calculate in Python if you want Excel formulas\n```\n\n### 3. Work with Existing Structure\n```python\n# Don't add/remove sheets unless necessary\n# Don't modify cell styles/colors\n# Only change values and formulas\n```\n\n### 4. Formula Copying Pattern\n```python\n# When copying formulas to multiple cells\nbase_formula = '=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D{}, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH({}$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))'\n\nfor row in range(12, 18):  # rows 12-17\n    for col in ['H', 'I', 'J', 'K', 'L']:\n        formula = base_formula.format(row, col)\n        sheet[f'{col}{row}'] = formula\n```\n\n## Error Handling\n```python\nfrom openpyxl import load_workbook\nfrom openpyxl.utils.exceptions import InvalidFileException\n\ntry:\n    wb = load_workbook('file.xlsx')\nexcept FileNotFoundError:\n    print(\"File not found\")\nexcept InvalidFileException:\n    print(\"Invalid Excel file\")\n```\n\n## Example: Complete Workflow\n```python\nfrom openpyxl import load_workbook\n\n# Load workbook\nwb = load_workbook('gdp.xlsx')\ntask_sheet = wb['Task']\ndata_sheet = wb['Data']\n\n# Write formulas\ntask_sheet['H12'] = '=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))'\n\n# Write statistical formulas\ntask_sheet['M35'] = '=MIN(H35:L35)'\ntask_sheet['M36'] = '=AVERAGE(H35:L35)'\n\n# Write weighted mean\ntask_sheet['H42'] = '=SUMPRODUCT(H35:L40, H7:L7) / SUM(H7:L7)'\n\n# Save and close\nwb.save('gdp.xlsx')\nwb.close()\n```\n\n## Common Issues\n- Formulas are stored as text, calculated when opened in Excel\n- Always use `=` prefix for formulas\n- Use proper Excel cell reference syntax\n- openpyxl doesn't calculate formulas (Excel does)\n- To see results, open file in Excel after saving\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}

Understanding GDP components and related metrics is essential for economic analysis.\n\n## GDP Components\n\n### Basic GDP Equation\n```\nGDP = C + I + G + (X - M)\n```\n\nWhere:\n- **C** = Consumption (household spending)\n- **I** = Investment (business investment)\n- **G** = Government spending\n- **X** = Exports\n- **M** = Imports\n- **(X - M)** = Net Exports (trade balance)\n\n## Net Exports\n\n### Definition\nNet exports represent the difference between a country's exports and imports.\n\n```\nNet Exports = Exports - Imports\n```\n\n### Net Exports as % of GDP\n```\nNet Exports (% of GDP) = [(Exports - Imports) / GDP] × 100\n```\n\n### Interpretation\n- **Positive Net Exports**: Trade surplus (exports > imports)\n- **Negative Net Exports**: Trade deficit (imports > exports)\n- **Zero Net Exports**: Trade balance\n\n## GCC Countries (Gulf Cooperation Council)\n\nThe GCC consists of six countries:\n1. **Bahrain** (BHR)\n2. **Kuwait** (KWT)\n3. **Oman** (OMN)\n4. **Qatar** (QAT)\n5. **Saudi Arabia** (SAU)\n6. **United Arab Emirates** (ARE)\n\nThese are oil-rich nations with historically strong export sectors.\n\n## Weighted Mean Calculation\n\n### Simple Mean vs Weighted Mean\n\n**Simple Mean:**\n```\nMean = (x₁ + x₂ + ... + xₙ) / n\n```\n\n**Weighted Mean:**\n```\nWeighted Mean = (x₁×w₁ + x₂×w₂ + ... + xₙ×wₙ) / (w₁ + w₂ + ... + wₙ)\n```\n\n### GDP-Weighted Mean\n\nWhen calculating regional averages, GDP is commonly used as the weight:\n\n```\nWeighted Mean = Σ(Country_Value × Country_GDP) / Σ(Country_GDP)\n```\n\n**Example:**\nIf calculating GCC's average net exports as % of GDP:\n```\nGCC_Weighted_Mean =\n  (Bahrain_NetExp% × Bahrain_GDP +\n   Kuwait_NetExp% × Kuwait_GDP +\n   ... +\n   UAE_NetExp% × UAE_GDP) /\n  (Sum of all GCC countries' GDP)\n```\n\n### Using SUMPRODUCT in Excel\n```excel\n=SUMPRODUCT(net_exports_range, gdp_range) / SUM(gdp_range)\n```\n\nWhere:\n- `net_exports_range`: Net exports as % of GDP for all countries\n- `gdp_range`: GDP values for all countries (weights)\n\n## Statistical Measures for Economic Analysis\n\n### Central Tendency\n- **Mean**: Average value (simple or weighted)\n- **Median**: Middle value (less affected by outliers)\n\n### Dispersion\n- **Min**: Lowest value\n- **Max**: Highest value\n- **Range**: Max - Min\n\n### Distribution\n- **25th Percentile (Q1)**: 25% of values are below this\n- **75th Percentile (Q3)**: 75% of values are below this\n- **Interquartile Range (IQR)**: Q3 - Q1\n\n## Series Codes (Common Economic Indicators)\n\nTypical series codes in economic data:\n- **NY.GDP.MKTP.CD**: GDP (current US$)\n- **NE.EXP.GNFS.ZS**: Exports of goods and services (% of GDP)\n- **NE.IMP.GNFS.ZS**: Imports of goods and services (% of GDP)\n- **NE.RSB.GNFS.ZS**: External balance on goods and services (% of GDP)\n- **GDP**: Gross Domestic Product\n- **EXP**: Exports\n- **IMP**: Imports\n\n## Practical Application\n\n### Step 1: Data Lookup\nRetrieve economic indicators using series codes and years:\n```\nINDEX(data_range, MATCH(series_code, codes, 0), MATCH(year, years, 0))\n```\n\n### Step 2: Calculate Net Exports\n```\nNet Exports % = Exports % - Imports %\n```\n\nOr use the external balance series if available.\n\n### Step 3: Calculate Statistics\nFor each year across all countries:\n- Min, Max, Median, Mean\n- 25th and 75th percentiles\n\n### Step 4: Weighted Mean\nUse GDP as weights:\n```\nSUMPRODUCT(net_exports_values, gdp_values) / SUM(gdp_values)\n```\n\n## Why Weighted Mean Matters\n\n**Example Scenario:**\n- Country A: Net Exports = 10%, GDP = $10 billion\n- Country B: Net Exports = 30%, GDP = $100 billion\n\n**Simple Mean:** (10% + 30%) / 2 = 20%\n\n**Weighted Mean:**\n(10% × 10 + 30% × 100) / (10 + 100) = 3100 / 110 = 28.2%\n\nThe weighted mean gives more importance to Country B because its economy is 10× larger, providing a more accurate regional picture.\n\n## Key Considerations\n\n1. **Currency**: Ensure GDP is in same currency across countries\n2. **Time Period**: Use same years for comparison\n3. **Data Quality**: Verify source and completeness\n4. **Weights**: GDP weights reflect economic size importance\n5. **Interpretation**: Weighted mean represents economic reality better than simple mean for regional aggregates\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}
{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_vrtx_01ES7wd8CfZgPwCmTgMW9Pms","type":"tool_result","content":"File created successfully at: /root/environment/skills/excel-cell-references.md"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"7e740bfe-08df-480e-b76c-6010c6a8e1ca","uuid":"f24f3dcf-5b42-48f7-985e-e14a50468226","tool_use_result":{"type":"create","filePath":"/root/environment/skills/excel-cell-references.md","content":"# Excel Cell References and Formula Patterns\n\n## Overview\nExcel cell references are fundamental to creating formulas. Understanding absolute, relative, and mixed references is crucial for building formulas that work correctly when copied to other cells.\n\n## Types of Cell References\n\n### 1. Relative Reference (A1)\nChanges when formula is copied to another cell.\n\n**Example:**\n```\nA1\n```\n\n**Behavior:**\n- Copied right: becomes B1, C1, D1, etc.\n- Copied down: becomes A2, A3, A4, etc.\n- Copied diagonally: both row and column adjust\n\n**Use Case:** When you want the reference to adjust relative to the new position.\n\n### 2. Absolute Reference ($A$1)\nStays fixed when formula is copied anywhere.\n\n**Example:**\n```\n$A$1\n```\n\n**Behavior:**\n- Always refers to cell A1, no matter where copied\n- Both row (1) and column (A) are locked\n\n**Use Case:** Referring to a constant value, fixed range, or lookup table.\n\n### 3. Mixed References ($A1 or A$1)\n\n**Column Absolute, Row Relative ($A1):**\n```\n$A1\n```\n- Column A is locked\n- Row changes when copied up/down\n- Useful for formulas copied down but referencing fixed column\n\n**Row Absolute, Column Relative (A$1):**\n```\nA$1\n```\n- Row 1 is locked\n- Column changes when copied left/right\n- Useful for formulas copied across but referencing fixed row\n\n## Formula Copying Patterns\n\n### Pattern 1: Two-Way Lookup Table\nCommon in data retrieval where rows and columns represent different criteria.\n\n**Structure:**\n```\n        H       I       J       K       L\n10            2019    2020    2021    2022    (Column headers)\n12   CODE1    ???     ???     ???     ???\n13   CODE2    ???     ???     ???     ???\n```\n\n**Formula for H12:**\n```\n=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))\n```\n\n**Breaking it down:**\n- `Data!$A$21:$F$40`: Absolute data range (never changes)\n- `$D12`: Column locked, row relative (D12, D13, D14 when copied down)\n- `Data!$A$21:$A$40`: Absolute lookup range for rows\n- `H$10`: Row locked, column relative (H10, I10, J10 when copied right)\n- `Data!$A$21:$F$21`: Absolute lookup range for columns\n\n**When copied:**\n- To I12: becomes `MATCH(I$10, ...)` - different year\n- To H13: becomes `MATCH($D13, ...)` - different code\n- To I13: both adjust - different year AND code\n\n### Pattern 2: Statistical Calculations Across Rows\nCalculating statistics for each row separately.\n\n**Formula for M35 (MIN):**\n```\n=MIN(H35:L35)\n```\n\n**When copied to M36:**\n```\n=MIN(H36:L36)\n```\n\n**Use:** All references are relative, so the formula adjusts to each row.\n\n### Pattern 3: SUMPRODUCT with Fixed Weights\nWeighted calculation where weights don't change.\n\n**Formula:**\n```\n=SUMPRODUCT(H35:L35, H$7:L$7) / SUM(H$7:L$7)\n```\n\n**Breaking it down:**\n- `H35:L35`: Fully relative (values row changes when copied)\n- `H$7:L$7`: Row locked (weights always from row 7)\n- `SUM(H$7:L$7)`: Same - row locked to 7\n\n**When copied to row 36:**\n```\n=SUMPRODUCT(H36:L36, H$7:L$7) / SUM(H$7:L$7)\n```\nValues row changes, weights stay fixed.\n\n## Cross-Sheet References\n\n### Syntax\n```\nSheetName!CellReference\n```\n\n**Examples:**\n```\nData!A1              ' Cell A1 in Data sheet\nData!$A$1            ' Absolute reference to A1 in Data sheet\nData!$A$21:$F$40     ' Absolute range in Data sheet\n'Sheet Name'!A1      ' Use quotes if sheet name has spaces\n```\n\n### Best Practice for Cross-Sheet Formulas\n```\n=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))\n```\n\n- Always use absolute references for ranges in other sheets\n- Use appropriate relative/absolute for criteria based on copy direction\n\n## Common Formula Patterns\n\n### 1. Lookup with Two Criteria (Row and Column)\n```\n=INDEX(absolute_data_range,\n       MATCH(row_criterion_mixed, absolute_row_range, 0),\n       MATCH(col_criterion_mixed, absolute_col_range, 0))\n```\n\n### 2. Statistical Functions for Horizontal Range\n```\n=MIN(relative_range)      ' Each row gets its own calculation\n=MAX(relative_range)\n=MEDIAN(relative_range)\n=AVERAGE(relative_range)\n```\n\n### 3. Percentile Calculations\n```\n=PERCENTILE.INC(relative_range, 0.25)   ' 25th percentile\n=PERCENTILE.INC(relative_range, 0.75)   ' 75th percentile\n```\n\n### 4. Weighted Mean with Fixed Weights\n```\n=SUMPRODUCT(relative_values, absolute_weights) / SUM(absolute_weights)\n```\n\n## Quick Reference Guide\n\n| Pattern | Example | Behavior When Copied |\n|---------|---------|----------------------|\n| Relative | A1 | Both row and column adjust |\n| Absolute | $A$1 | Nothing changes |\n| Column Locked | $A1 | Column fixed, row adjusts |\n| Row Locked | A$1 | Row fixed, column adjusts |\n| Range Absolute | $A$1:$B$10 | Range stays fixed |\n| Cross-sheet | Data!$A$1 | Sheet reference maintained |\n\n## Practical Example: Building a Formula Grid\n\n**Scenario:** Fill H12:L17 with lookups based on column D (codes) and row 10 (years).\n\n**Step 1 - Write formula for H12:**\n```\n=INDEX(Data!$A$21:$F$40, MATCH($D12, Data!$A$21:$A$40, 0), MATCH(H$10, Data!$A$21:$F$21, 0))\n```\n\n**Step 2 - Copy across to I12:L12:**\n- H$10 becomes I$10, J$10, K$10, L$10 (different years)\n- $D12 stays as $D12 (same row code)\n\n**Step 3 - Copy entire row down to rows 13-17:**\n- $D12 becomes $D13, $D14, $D15, etc. (different codes)\n- Column references maintain their pattern\n\n**Result:** Full grid automatically populated with correct lookups.\n\n## Debugging Tips\n\n1. **Formula Auditing:**\n   - Click on cell to see formula in formula bar\n   - Check if $ symbols are in right places\n   - Verify sheet references\n\n2. **Trace Precedents:**\n   - Use Excel's \"Trace Precedents\" feature\n   - Verify cells being referenced\n\n3. **Test Pattern:**\n   - Write formula for one cell\n   - Copy to adjacent cell\n   - Check if references adjusted correctly\n   - If correct, copy to entire range\n\n4. **Common Errors:**\n   - `#REF!`: Invalid cell reference (often from incorrect absolute/relative)\n   - `#N/A`: MATCH couldn't find value (check criteria)\n   - `#VALUE!`: Wrong data type in formula\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}
